## Divergences

| Python file | Line | C++ reference | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|
| pymoses/comboreduct/combo/assumption.py | 12 | C++ moses/comboreduct/combo/assumption.cc, whole file | no forest -> plain list. `util/tree.py` deliberately does not support that forest structure -- "no forests/multiple roots (no head/feet sentinels)" is stated in tree.py's own module docstring, is enforced at runtime (`insert`/`insert_after`/`insert_subtree`/`insert_subtree_after`/ `move_after`/`move_before`/`move_ontop` all raise `ValueError` if asked to give the tree's own root a sibling), and is regression-tested (`test_insert_after_on_the_tree_root_is_rejected`, tests/util/test_tree.py) as a decision already made in the prior (S0-S6) cycle. `combo/iostream_combo.py`'s `str_to_combo_tree` independently enforces the same thing at the text-format level ("a combo_tree is always single-rooted... rejects trailing content"). None of this is something Task 1 can or should reopen -- CARRY-FORWARD.md section 5 records "'forests unsupported'... is uniform" as an accepted, load-bearing property of this whole port, not an oversight. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/combo/descriptions.py | 195 | C++ moses/comboreduct/combo/descriptions.h:56 | classic indexes a fixed `type_tree argument[builtin_count][3]` array, so an `i` past the number of inputs actually stored reads a default-constructed (EMPTY) `type_tree` rather than running off the end -- but an `i >= maximal_builtin_arity` IS out of bounds and is undefined behaviour there. Here the stored inputs are a list, so an index past the end returns the same empty `type_tree` for the in-bounds case and raises `IndexError` for the out-of-bounds one instead of reading adjacent memory. No caller reaches either: `get_input_type_tree(builtin, i)` (type_tree.cc:65) only calls this with `i == 0` or with `i < builtin_arity(b) <= maximal_builtin_arity`. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/combo/iostream_combo.py | 431 | C++ moses/comboreduct/combo/iostream_combo.cc:384-424 | NOT a divergence in this port -- recorded here because it is easy to get wrong by analogy with `_combo_it_to_str`/`ostream_combo_it` (iostream_combo.h:84-102), which DOES explicitly merge `output_format::python` and `::python3` into one `case`. This function's own C++ switch on `fmt` has exactly THREE cases -- `combo`, `python`, `scheme` -- each ending in `return`, with NO `case output_format::python3:` label at all; `python3` therefore falls to the switch's own `default:` (iostream_combo.cc:423-424), `"Format " << static_cast<unsigned>(fmt) << "unknown"` (note: NO space before "unknown" here, unlike `ostream_builtin`'s otherwise near-identical default two functions down -- a real, verified inconsistency between the two, not a transcription slip in this docstring). Ported below by falling through to the same `_UNKNOWN_FORMAT` branch `scheme` printing's own unported case does NOT take -- `python3` is not "unimplemented", it behaves exactly like any other value C++ itself does not recognise in this particular function. Unreachable from any real CLI run either way: `parse_output_format` (this module) never returns `python3` (see its own docstring), so no port test can reach this path from `moses_exec.py`; included for a caller that constructs `output_format.python3` directly. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/combo/similarity.py | 303 | C++ moses/comboreduct/combo/similarity.h:44-49 | classic defines this function's BODY directly in the header, with no `inline` keyword -- a real ODR violation waiting to happen the moment more than one translation unit includes similarity.h (none currently does; only similarity.cc does). Ported as an ordinary Python function; Python has no translation-unit/ODR concept for this to matter, so there is nothing to reproduce or diverge from behaviourally -- noted here only because it is a genuine defect in the header worth knowing about if this file is ever touched again. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/combo/vertex.py | 92 | C++ moses/comboreduct/combo/vertex.h:98 | `lambda` is a hard Python keyword -- it starts a lambda expression grammatically, so `lambda = 23` is a SyntaxError and cannot appear as a class-body assignment target the way every other member here does. Renamed `lambda_`, the standard PEP 8 convention for exactly this collision (compare `class_`, `type_` elsewhere in the Python ecosystem). Only the Python identifier is affected: the combo surface syntax this builtin maps to/from ("->" and "lambda", both plain string literals, see iostream_combo.py) is unchanged. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/eval.py | 150 | C++ eval.cc:124 | C++'s vertex_seq is std::vector<vertex>, and operator[] on an out-of-range index is undefined behaviour, not a throw -- Python's list[idx] raising IndexError here is strictly safer than that UB, not a behaviour C++ specifically guarantees. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/eval.py | 228 | C++ eval.cc:217 | `x / y` in C++ is IEEE754 float division -- a zero divisor produces a signed infinity, not an exception. Python's `float / float` raises ZeroDivisionError for a zero divisor instead, so the signed-infinity result is reconstructed by hand from the operands' signs (x is already known nonzero here). |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/eval.py | 250 | C++ eval.cc:228 | `log(0)`/`log(negative)` are IEEE754 `-inf`/`nan` in C++; Python's `math.log` raises ValueError for both instead, so both are reconstructed by hand. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/eval.py | 264 | C++ eval.cc:237 | C++'s exp() overflows to IEEE754 +inf for a too-large argument; Python's math.exp raises OverflowError instead (exp() is never nan for a finite real argument, matching C++'s own isinf-only check here). |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/eval.py | 279 | C++ eval.cc:246 | C's sin(inf)/sin(nan) is nan, silently -- eval.cc itself never isnan/ isinf-checks sin()'s result the way log/exp/div above do, so a nan here is a plain, valid returned vertex, not raised. Python's math.sin instead raises ValueError for a non-finite argument (task-1 review, Minor: a bare ValueError here would have been indistinguishable from car/cdr's "not a list!" ValueError below -- returning nan instead removes the ambiguity by removing the exception). |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/interpreter.py | 163 | C++ moses/comboreduct/interpreter/interpreter.cc:54 | DO NOT "FIX" BY MATCHING CLASSIC. C++ indexes a `std::vector` with a bare `operator[]` and no bounds check, so an argument beyond the table's arity is UNDEFINED BEHAVIOUR there -- it reads past the end. Measured on a 2-input boolean table with `-e 'and($1 $99)'`: classic prints `0 and($1 $99)` and exits 0 -- a PERFECT score for a tree it cannot evaluate -- and the same command with `-W1` segfaults (rc 139) instead, deterministically, five runs each way. A port cannot faithfully reproduce undefined behaviour, and reproducing the "perfect score" would be worse than failing: it would silently rank an ill-formed exemplar first. Python's list indexing raises `IndexError` here already; this makes the failure say what actually happened, since a bare `IndexError: list index out of range` from inside a recursive evaluator gives the user nothing to act on. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/interpreter.py | 374 | C++ interpreter.cc:164 | `x / y` in C++ is IEEE754 float division -- a zero divisor produces a signed infinity, not an exception. Python's `float / float` raises ZeroDivisionError for a zero divisor instead, so the signed-infinity result is reconstructed by hand from the operands' signs (`x` is already known nonzero here). Same reconstruction `eval.py`'s `eval_throws_binding` uses for its own (separate) `div` case -- eval.cc:217 -- kept as an independent copy here per this module's own "each mirrors its own C++ source file" convention (see eval.py's module docstring). |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/interpreter.py | 405 | C++ interpreter.cc:175 | `log(0)`/ `log(negative)` are IEEE754 `-inf`/`nan` in C++; Python's `math.log` raises `ValueError` for both instead, so both are reconstructed by hand -- same shape as eval.py's `log` case. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/interpreter.py | 424 | C++ interpreter.cc:183 | C's exp() overflows to IEEE754 +inf for a too-large argument; Python's `math.exp` raises `OverflowError` instead. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/interpreter/interpreter.py | 440 | C++ interpreter.cc:191 | Python's `math.sin` raises `ValueError` for a non-finite argument instead of returning `nan`; reconstructed by hand, matching eval.py's `sin` case (eval.cc:246) exactly. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/reduct/branch_rules.py | 258 | C++ branch_rules.cc:139-140 | C++'s trailing cleanup loop (`for (; sib != it.end(); ++sib) { tr.erase(sib); num--; }`) never reassigns `sib` from `tr.erase`'s own return value -- it erases the CURRENT node, then reads `++sib` (`sib->next_sibling`) off the iterator's OWN copy of the now-freed node pointer. Real C++ relies on that read landing on still-intact (if technically-freed) memory -- undefined behaviour that happens to work in practice, not a documented guarantee. This port's `tree.erase` POISONS the erased node's own `next_sibling` with a `_DEAD` sentinel specifically so a stale handle fails LOUDLY (`AttributeError`) rather than silently reading freed memory -- a literal transliteration would crash every time this branch runs. The successor is captured BEFORE erasing instead (`nxt = sib.next_sibling` then `tr.erase(sib)` then `sib = nxt`), reproducing the code's plain observable intent (erase every child from here through the end) rather than its undefined-behaviour mechanism -- the two agree on every input that does not itself depend on freed-memory contents, which none of this rule's real callers do. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/reduct/flat_normal_form.py | 299 | C++ flat_normal_form.h:53 | yields via a generator, not an output iterator. C++'s `template<typename Out> void implications(c1, c2, Out out)` writes each resolvent via `(*out++) = c`, taking whatever output-iterator adapter the caller passes; this port drops the `out` parameter and `yield`s each resolvent instead. Every real call site (`logical_rules.cc:313,375`, Task 4) uses `std::back_inserter` into a fresh `nf` -- `list(implications(c1, c2))` here reproduces exactly that, and no other output-iterator shape is used anywhere in $CLASSIC, so the collapse changes no real caller's observable result -- only the C++ TEMPLATE PARAMETER this port has no equivalent mechanism for. |
| pymoses/comboreduct/reduct/flat_normal_form.py | 529 | C++ flat_normal_form.h:124 | takes a node, not a (from,to) sibling sub-range. C++'s `add_cnf(sib_it from, sib_it to)` accepts an arbitrary sibling sub-range; this port narrows the parameter to `node` (its FULL child range only) because that is the only shape the real call site (`logical_rules.cc:348`, Task 4: `tr.begin(it), tr.end(it)` -- `it`'s complete child list) ever uses -- the same scoping precedent this port's `tree.sort`/`tree.partition`/`tree.sort_on_subtrees` already established. Every real call site takes a FRESH snapshot list of a live sibling range immediately before calling one of these -- behaviourally equivalent to a live counting_iterator walk here, because each algorithm below only ever reads forward and erases/inserts at or before its current scan position: an erasure can only remove an element the scan has already passed or is standing on (never one still ahead relative to the OTHER range's pointer), and an insertion via `tree_inserter` either lands before the destination range's CURRENT element (leaving that element's own future traversal, and hence the snapshot's remaining entries, unaffected) or strictly past the snapshotted range's end (an append, which the snapshot -- bounded at the range's original length -- was never going to walk into anyway). --------------------------------------------------------------------------- |

| pymoses/comboreduct/reduct/reduct.py | 152 | C++ moses/comboreduct/reduct/reduct.h:63-68 crule<T> | not ported as its own class -- see reduct.py's module docstring for the zero-call-site check on the other two reduct.h omissions; this one is a design collapse, not a zero-call-site absence, so it gets its own explanation here. `crule<T>`'s entire C++ purpose is generating a type-correct `clone()` override per subclass via CRTP, so that a `shared_ptr<const rule>`-owning container (each meta-rule's own wrapped-rule member; `sequential.rules`, a `boost::ptr_vector<rule>`) gets an INDEPENDENT COPY on construction, protecting against the ORIGINAL rule object's own lifetime or later mutation. Neither hazard exists in Python: objects are garbage collected (no dangling-owner concern), and no rule anywhere in `meta_rules.cc` ever mutates its own or another rule's fields after construction -- every `operator()` body reads `r`/`rules` only to CALL through them (`(*r)(tr, it)`, `r(tr, it)`), never assigns through them. A single generic `clone()` here, shared by every subclass via inheritance, is therefore behaviourally equivalent to CRTP-generated per-subclass clones: it returns a NEW object (`copy.copy`, never `self` -- `clone() is not self` holds, matching "a new instance" being the one thing C++'s `new T(*this)` guarantees that a caller can actually observe). Only the object IDENTITY of a clone's own NESTED `.r`/`.rules` differs from a hypothetical recursive re-clone (shared by reference here, `copy.copy` being shallow, rather than independently re-cloned) -- unobservable, for the same never-mutated-through-a-reference reason. |

It is worse than non-deterministic-looking: WHICH observation vanishes changes when the CTable is COPIED, and `table-problems.cc` copies it twice before scoring, so the table classic actually scores (5 true / 3 false) differs from the one `Table::compressed` returned (6 true / 2 false). A port cannot faithfully reproduce that: it would mean emulating libstdc++'s red-black tree, its rebalancing, AND its copy constructor, under a comparator the standard says makes the result undefined. This port therefore computes the INTENDED result -- every distinct `(value, timestamp)` observation retained with its full count, which is exactly the key set C++'s own equivalence relation (`!(a<b) && !(b<a)`, member-wise equality) defines. What a user sees, concretely: on a `--timestamp-feature` table where some compressed row holds observations whose value order and date order disagree, classic drops or duplicates observations and this port does not. Scores computed from that table differ accordingly (measured: classic `-Hpre` reports 0.625 on timestamp_bool_dup.csv where all nine rows would give 6/9). On a table with no such row -- which includes EVERY table with no timestamp column at all, since every observation then carries the same sentinel date -- the two agree exactly, and `tests/fixtures/table_data/ timestamp_bool_clean.csv` is the corpus member that proves it. |

Through either of them a table's timestamps are LOST -- `get_timestamps()` on the result returns the empty set. That is the port's behaviour from before the timestamp slice, so it is a non-regression rather than a new gap. Raising here instead was tried and rejected: it breaks both callers outright, in trees this task does not own. `scoring_base._copy_ctable` was a THIRD such place, and the worst of them -- it rebuilt each cell as a plain `collections.Counter`, so `bscore_ctable_base._wrk_ctable` lost every timestamp the loader had just parsed. Only the bscore's LENGTH changes, and the one CLI surface that would show it, `-t/--output-bscore`, is separately gated in `merging.py`. Confirmed by running this port with a faithful `filtered_preserve_idxs`/`ignore_cols` monkeypatched in and with it stubbed to a no-op: byte-identical stdout on every dataset x --fs-algo x --fs-target-size x seed cell of the oracle matrix, and identical to classic's own binary. | pymoses/moses/deme/deme_params.py | 35 | C++ moses/moses/deme/deme_params.h:54,82 | ``max_candidates_per_ deme`` and ``perm_ratio`` are declared as plain ``int``/``float`` members with NO default-member-initializer, and the constructor's own initializer list (deme_params.h:44-50) never touches either -- so a freshly-constructed C++ ``deme_parameters`` genuinely leaves both fields holding INDETERMINATE (uninitialized stack) values. This is the same shape ``metapop_params.py`` already documents twice (``subsample_deme_filter_parameters``'s ten fields, ``diversity_parameters.autoscale``): Python has no equivalent of an uninitialized primitive, so rather than invent a plausible default (which would silently mask "this was never set" the way a stray ``0.0``/``-1`` could) this port simply never sets these two attributes -- reading one before an explicit assignment raises ``AttributeError`` instead of returning C++'s indeterminate garbage. UNLIKE those two precedents, though, ``perm_ratio`` is NOT behind a P2 gate: ``deme_expander.create_representations`` reads ``_params.perm_ratio`` UNCONDITIONALLY on every deme built (deme_expander.cc:294, passed straight into every ``representation(...)`` call) -- reachable on the DEFAULT path, not merely a dormant field guarded by some other flag. This is a caller obligation in C++ too, not a gap this port introduces: every real classic caller sets it explicitly before constructing a ``deme_expander`` -- ``problem-params.cc:2265``, ``deme_params.perm_ratio = perm_ratio;`` (CLI default value ``0.0``), unconditionally, on every run. Whoever drives this port's ``deme_expander`` (a test, or the S13 CLI wiring once it exists) MUST set ``.perm_ratio`` before calling ``create_demes``/ ``create_representations`` -- and per ``build_knobs.py``'s own ``_validate_request``, S8's ``representation``/``build_knobs`` accept ONLY exactly ``-1.0`` (the classic early-return "no pair sampling" mode), so ``-1.0`` is also the only value this port's ``deme_expander`` can actually drive to completion without raising ``NotImplementedError`` itself (build_knobs.py's own gate, not this file's). CORRECTED (S10 branch review, Minor): this comment used to claim that whoever reads a ``deme_parameters.max_candidates_per_deme`` would be ``metapopulation``/``merging`` -- MEASURED FALSE. ``deme_params.h:54``'s field is never read ANYWHERE in classic (confirmed: zero references beyond its own declaration and the equally-dead ``deme_expander.cc``, which also never reads it -- zero references in that 533-line file either). ``merging.cc:536``'s ``_params.max_candidates_per_deme`` reads a DIFFERENT field on a DIFFERENT struct: ``metapopulation``'s own ``_params``, of type ``metapop_parameters`` (declared separately, at ``metapop_params.h:183``, and ported here as ``metapop_params.py``'s own ``metapop_parameters.max_candidates_per_deme``) -- the two fields merely share a name and a constructor default (``-1``) by coincidence of both authors solving the same "cap candidates per deme" problem independently; they are not the same storage, and nothing in classic ever copies one into the other. ``deme_params.h:54`` is therefore genuinely dead in classic, not merely dead in this port's own scope. Observed by inspection, NOT measured (no probe was run to confirm this is live UB rather than a technically-legal-but-surprising lifetime): both ``_params``/``_filter_params`` on ``deme_expander`` (deme_expander.h:150, 152) are ``const&`` members bound, via the constructor's initializer list, from constructor PARAMETERS (``pa``, ``fp``) that default to temporaries -- ``deme_parameters()``, ``subsample_deme_filter_parameters()`` (deme_expander.h:45-46) -- built at the call site. A reference data member initialized from a reference parameter does not itself extend a temporary's lifetime past the end of the constructor call's full expression, so a caller that constructs a ``deme_expander`` WITHOUT supplying either argument binds ``_params``/``_filter_params`` to already-destroyed temporaries for the rest of the object's life. Whether this is actually reachable is not established here: the only real construction site found in classic, ``moses_main.h:318`` (``deme_expander dex(tt, si_ca, si_kb, sc, *optimizer, deme_params, filter_params);``), supplies both arguments explicitly, every time -- so if this is live UB, it is dormant in every production code path this search covered. Python objects carry no such reference-lifetime hazard: this port's own ``deme_expander.__init__`` default (a freshly-constructed ``deme_parameters()``/``subsample_deme_filter_parameters()`` per call, ordinary garbage-collected objects) is actually SAFE where C++'s equivalent default argument may not be -- kept only for constructor- signature parity with C++, not because any caller here (or, per the above, in classic) is ever observed relying on it. |

| pymoses/moses/metapopulation/ensemble.py | 287 | C++ moses/moses/metapopulation/ensemble.cc:98 | classic picks the best candidate with `std::min_element` over `cands`, a `scored_combo_tree_set` -- which is a `std::unordered_set` (types.h:370). `min_element` keeps the FIRST element satisfying the comparator, so **on a tie the winner is decided by hash-bucket iteration order**, which the C++ standard does not specify. The loop below is a faithful `min_element`: `>` keeps the first maximum, exactly as classic's comparator does. What it cannot reproduce is which element comes first. Ties are not rare here -- MEASURED on `-Hmux -k2 --boost=1 -m1000 -r1`: 4 of 4 candidates tied at the top score on the first call, and 12 of 32 on the second. So the choice is real, and the consequence compounds: a different first member changes every subsequent row weight, hence the whole ensemble. Across 18 boosting runs, 11 agree with classic exactly and 7 differ in score, sometimes in the port's favour (-2 where classic gets -3), which is what rules out a systematic error in the arithmetic. DO NOT "fix" by sorting: any total order imposed here would be this port inventing a rule classic does not have. See the `scored_combo_tree_set` DIVERGENCE in types.py for the same issue in its container form. |

| pymoses/moses/moses/local_moses.py | 62 | C++ moses/moses/moses/local_moses.cc:120-122,147-151 | This is a CLOCK CLASS change, not merely a resolution change. `gettimeofday()` reads `CLOCK_REALTIME` -- the settable wall clock, which NTP (or a manual `date`/`settimeofday` call) can step forward OR backward at any instant, including mid-run. `time.monotonic()` reads a monotonic clock (`CLOCK_MONOTONIC` on Linux) that is immune to those steps and is documented never to go backward. Resolution is a real, secondary difference too (`gettimeofday`'s microsecond vs. whatever the host monotonic clock offers, typically nanosecond on Linux -- strictly finer, never coarser), but the load-bearing difference is what each clock TRACKS: a real NTP step during a `local_moses` run could make C++'s own `timersub`-computed `elapsed_secs` jump or even go negative for one generation; this port's `time.monotonic()`-based accumulation cannot, by construction. `not_max_time()` DOES decide a termination in this port's own test suite: `test_local_moses.py`'s `test_not_max_time_blocks_the_loop` sets `pa.max_time = 0` for exactly that purpose, and three more tests in that file (`test_local_moses_resets_elapsed_secs_unconditionally_before_the_ loop`, `test_local_moses_carries_start_forward_each_iteration_not_ quadratic`, `test_local_moses_truncates_max_time_argument_toward_zero`) drive `stats.elapsed_secs`'s own accumulation directly. What remains genuinely untested is narrower than "never decides a termination": no test here runs `local_moses` across multiple REAL-clock-timed iterations against a nonzero budget it crosses mid-loop, so the REALTIME-vs- MONOTONIC clock-class difference above -- its behaviour under an actual clock step during a live run -- has no test of its own, and `pa.max_time` defaulting to `_INT32_MAX` seconds (moses_params.py) means no test that exercises a real timed run needs to think about that budget either. |

Reproducing boost's `beta` bit-for-bit is a substantial numeric port of its own, and this branch needs a contin field of depth > 170 -- i.e. `--contin-depth 171` or higher, a search space of 2**171 settings -- to be reached at all. Gated rather than approximated: a silently-close number here would be indistinguishable from a correct one. |

| pymoses/moses/moses/optimization/hill_climbing.py | 839 | C++ moses/moses/optimization/hill-climbing.cc:687 | DO NOT FIX -- the innermost loop is `for (unsigned k = 0; k < i; k++)` while the index formula on the very next line assumes `k < j`. So `n` is not a bijection, and this is what actually happens (derived from the formula and confirmed against tests/fixtures/crossover.json, `cross_top_three`'s own cases): * NOTHING IS SKIPPED. For a fixed `i` the offsets `j*(j-1)/2 + k` produced are the union over j in [1,i) of the i consecutive values [j*(j-1)/2, j*(j-1)/2 + i). Successive j-runs start j apart with j < i, so the runs overlap and the union is the CONTIGUOUS range [0, i*(i-1)/2]. The intended range for block i is [0, i*(i-1)/2), so the block covers all of its own slots and then one more. * SLOTS ARE WRITTEN REPEATEDLY, last write wins. Offset m in block i is written once for every j in [1,i) with 0 <= m - j*(j-1)/2 < i, which is up to O(sqrt(i)) times. Only the final (j, k) pair survives, and it is not the (j, k) the formula intends: e.g. i=3 writes n=3 twice, first as (j=1, k=2) and then as (j=2, k=1), and the second wins. * ONE SLOT OF THE NEXT BLOCK IS CLOBBERED EARLY. The block's last offset, i*(i-1)/2, maps to n = C(i+1,3), i.e. block i+1's slot 0, which block i+1 then rewrites with its own (j=1, k=0). * `k == i` NEVER HAPPENS -- the loop bound is `k < i`, strictly, so `i` itself is never a value `k` takes. The only degenerate case is `k == j` (k ranges over [0, i) and j over [1, i), so k == j occurs exactly once per (i, j) sweep). When it does, `src = deme[sample_start + k]` is the SAME sample as `jref` (both read `deme[sample_start + j]`), so `child` starts as a copy of jref, not a fresh three-way mix. * `merge_instance(child, base, jref)` -- the SECOND merge -- is NOT a no-op here; it OVERWRITES whatever the first merge (`merge_instance(child, base, iref)`) left behind, at every knob where `base != jref`, resetting those knobs to jref's own values. Measured with the port's own `field_set.merge_instance` on three ternary knobs (MOSES's actual logical knob, multy=3): base=[0,0,0], iref=[1,1,1], jref=[2,2,2] -- `child` goes [2,2,2] (copy of jref) -> [1,1,1] (merge with iref) -> [2,2,2] (merge with jref). The net effect in this configuration is sample j UNCHANGED, not a two-way cross: the k == j slot's "three-way cross" degenerates to writing back the original jref sample verbatim, because base disagrees with jref at every knob and the second merge therefore restores all of them. * `if (num_to_make <= n) return num_to_make;` (:689) still leaves no hole below `num_to_make`: because the covered offsets always form a contiguous prefix and rise by one within each j-run, the first `n >= num_to_make` is reached only after every smaller slot has already been written at least once. Net effect: the function returns the promised count of initialized instances, but their CONTENT is a different multiset of crosses than `k < j` would produce. Reproduced literally, because bit-identical behaviour against classic is the point. The `unsigned` truncations are the same family `cross_top_two` documents. This one is the most reachable of them, but the onset is i == 1627, not i == 2953 as an earlier version of this note claimed (understating it by ~1.8x): `i`, `j`, `k` are themselves `unsigned` (32-bit) in C++, so `i * (i - 1) * (i - 2)` wraps mod 2**32 AT THE MULTIPLICATION, before the `/ 6` -- and the raw product first exceeds 2**32 at i == 1627 (1627*1626*1625 == 4,298,940,750; i == 1626 gives 4,291,014,000, still under). i == 2953 was instead (an incorrect) attempt at the DIFFERENT threshold where the unwrapped quotient `i*(i-1)*(i-2)/6` itself would cross 2**32 -- that is i == 2955, not 2953 (i == 2954's quotient is 4,291,795,704, still under) -- but that quotient threshold was never the relevant one anyway, since the 32-bit wraparound in C++ happens two steps earlier, in the product itself. masked, never clamped. |
| pymoses/moses/moses/optimization/hill_climbing.py | 1082 | C++ moses/moses/optimization/hill-climbing.cc:815-819 | this used to be `deme.sort(reverse=True)`, on the argument that a stable sort "can only be at least as well-ordered as" an unspecified one, so there was no C++ tie order to match. That argument is FALSE and was withdrawn in S12 after measuring libstdc++ directly: `std::partial_sort` with ties present returns a genuinely different permutation from a stable sort (`all_tied_8_mid4` measures as [1,3,0,2,...] where a stable sort gives [0,1,2,3,...]), and since the very next line TRUNCATES the deme, tie order decides which tied instances survive. `_partial_sort` reproduces libstdc++ exactly; see its block comment. The one thing not reproduced is C++'s out-of-range `middle`: classic passes `next(deme.begin(), max_allowed_instances)` unconditionally, so when the RAM-pressure arm fires on a deme SMALLER than `max_allowed_instances` (the common case -- the default is 10000), `middle` is past `end()` and the call is undefined behaviour. Python clamps `middle` to `len(deme)`, which is what a full sort of the whole deme does and what the `erase` on the next line then makes a no-op -- the observable outcome any sane libstdc++ build produces, without pretending the C++ is defined. |

Skipping even one narrowing reintroduces double-precision comparisons where C++ has float32 ones, which changes how often `composite_score.__lt__`'s tie-break-by-complexity branch fires and, with it, which candidate exemplar selection prefers -- see the 20,656-pair discrepancy demonstrated above. |

Either way, this port's `vertex` union has no equivalent alternative for either constant. None of that is reproducible from Python, and -- UNLIKE `score_t` narrowing (this module's own top-of-file DIVERGENCE marker) -- it does not need to be: this hash value is never compared against a literal oracle value anywhere, only used internally by `scored_combo_tree_set`'s hash-bucketing (below), whose only real behavioural requirement is hash/equality CONSISTENCY -- `a == b` under `scored_combo_tree_equal` must imply `hash(a) == hash(b)` -- which this satisfies by construction. `std::unordered_set` itself makes no iteration-order promise in C++ either, so no observable behaviour depends on the literal value. The `builtin`/`argument` cases below need no such divergence: neither one calls `boost::hash_value` in C++ at all, so both are ported as exact integer arithmetic. |

| pymoses/moses/representation/build_knobs.py | 552 | C++ moses/moses/representation/build_knobs.cc:270-276 | classic walks the post-order iterator LIVE, erasing through `_exemplar.erase(it++)` as it goes -- per the S0-S6 plan's explicit "do not fix it by snapshotting" rule, that is normally something this port must not paper over. This snapshots the post-order sequence into a plain list up front instead, then re-checks `is_childless` fresh at each node during the loop (not a stale value captured at snapshot time). The two are behaviourally equivalent here specifically because only CHILDLESS nodes are ever erased: post-order guarantees every node is visited strictly after all of its own descendants, so an erased node can never be an ancestor of a later-visited node in this same snapshot, and a parent that becomes childless only because an earlier sibling/child was erased is still observed correctly, since its childless-ness is re-tested live against the real tree, not against the snapshot. Consequence: the `self._exemplar.is_valid(node)` guard below can never actually fire -- no snapshot entry is ever erased before its own turn -- and mutating it away (deleting the conjunct entirely) still leaves the test suite green. Kept anyway as the guard classic gets for free from `it++` capturing the successor before erasing. |
| pymoses/moses/representation/build_knobs.py | 610 | C++ moses/moses/representation/build_knobs.cc:311-341 | opt-in only (`--knob-probe-baseline reduced`; the default `classic` runs `disc_probe` above, unchanged). Classic disallows a setting when reducing the whole decorated tree with that setting lowers its complexity, which assumes the tree with every knob at 0 (the baseline) is a fixpoint of the knob-building reducer. Effort-2 logical reduction is not confluent, though: once `build_logical` has wrapped a literal in a single-child flip junctor, the reducer can find a simplification of the BASELINE it missed on the plain exemplar, and from then on every probe of every later knob "reduces", so almost every later knob is thrown away (docs/gomea/BACKLOG.md section 4 item 1; e.g. 135 knobs instead of about 1,000). This variant charges a setting only for the reduction it ADDS: it is disallowed when the setting's tree loses more complexity under reduction than the baseline does, c(T_idx) - c(R(T_idx)) > c(T_0) - c(R(T_0)). When the baseline is a fixpoint (c(T_0) == c(R(T_0)), the normal case) this is exactly classic's test, so it differs from classic only on demes whose baseline is itself reducible. |

What a user observes, MEASURED 2026-09-10 with `-Hpre -q0.5 -w1.0 --timestamp-feature=when --time-dispersion-pressure=0.5 -m300 -W1 -r1`: * tests/fixtures/table_data/timestamp_bool_clean.csv -- classic SIGSEGVs (rc 139, core dumped, no diagnostic), at every one of -m100/-m300/-m1000 crossed with -r1/-r7/-r42. gdb puts the fault in `representation::get_candidate_rec`'s tree-node allocation, NOT in any timestamp code: the corrupted red-black tree damages the heap and an unrelated later allocation is what dies. This port completes, printing a full 10-candidate listing headed `0.884651064872741699 !$i1`. * timestamp_bool_dup.csv -- classic completes, printing the one line `0.632471561431884766 true`; this port prints a 10-candidate listing headed `0.981214404106140137 !$i2`, differing for the SAME reason the UN-flagged run on that file already differs (its CTable cell is corrupted before scoring ever starts, so this is not a new divergence). Adding `--time-bscore-granularity=month` to either case collapses every date into one class, which removes the conflict and makes classic well defined again: both then print `nan true` (Hmax = log2(1) == 0, so the penalty divides by zero -- and see `_powf`, time_dispersion.py, for why that NaN prints POSITIVE). An UNtimestamped table is the other conflict-free family, and there this port is byte-identical to classic: measured over 7 datasets x {-m300, -m1000} x {-r1, -r5} x 7 flag combinations, 168/168 identical (tests/moses/main/test_time_bscore_cli.py). |

| pymoses/util/digraph.py | 8 | C++ opencog/util/digraph.h:96 | `randomized_topological_sort` shuffles its ready-node queue with a BARE `std::random_shuffle(nodes.begin(), nodes.end())` -- no RNG argument, so it does NOT read `randGen()`. That stream is reproduced here rather than substituted; the residual divergence is only that a C library's global state is reproduced by a Python object, and the reproduction is HOST-SPECIFIC (glibc + libstdc++, see below). The chain of measured facts, each re-derived on 2026-09-10 and pinned by `tests/fixtures/univariate_rng.json` (captured by `tools/capture_univariate_rng.py` from `tools/cpp_probe/univariate_rng_probe.cc`): 1. libstdc++'s two-argument `std::random_shuffle` is `for (i = first+1; i != last; ++i) { j = first + rand() % ((i-first)+1); if (i != j) iter_swap(i, j); }` (`/usr/include/c++/16/bits/stl_algo.h`, the `random_shuffle(_RandomAccessIterator, _RandomAccessIterator)` overload). It has a second, xorshift-seeded arm guarded by `#if RAND_MAX < __INT_MAX__`; glibc's `RAND_MAX` is `2147483647` (`/usr/include/stdlib.h:91`) which IS `__INT_MAX__`, so that arm is not even compiled here. `_random_shuffle` below is the surviving loop, transliterated. 2. `git grep 'srand(' $CLASSIC $COGUTIL` finds ZERO call sites, so classic consumes `rand()` from the state glibc statically initialises it to. `opencog::randGen().seed(rand_seed)` (problem-params.cc:2116, the `-r` flag) reseeds ONLY the mt19937 singleton; it never touches `rand()`. digraph.h:96 is also the ONLY `rand()`/`random_shuffle` call site in either checkout, so nothing else perturbs the stream. 3. glibc's static default state IS the state `srand(1)` produces: `stdlib/random_r.c` initialises `unsafe_state` to point into the TYPE_3 table `randtbl`, and `__srandom_r` maps `seed == 0` to 1. MEASURED by the probe's `rand_default` / `rand_srand1` / `rand_srand0` sections: 512 values, identical, and seed 0 == seed 1. 4. glibc's `rand()` is `__random()`, i.e. the TYPE_3 additive-feedback generator: state of `DEG_3 == 31` `int32_t`s, `SEP_3 == 3`, seeded by the Lehmer recurrence `state[i] = (16807 * state[i-1]) % 2147483647` evaluated via Schrage's trick, then `31 * 10 == 310` warm-up draws discarded; each draw is `*fptr += (uint32_t)*rptr` with the result `>> 1` ("chucking least random bit"). Source: glibc `stdlib/random_r.c` (`__srandom_r`, `__random_r`) and `stdlib/random.c`. `_CStdlibRand` below transliterates exactly that, and the fixture pins it element for element against the real `std::rand()`. What remains divergent, and cannot be otherwise: `-r <seed>` does not and cannot control this stream in classic either -- it is a property of the glibc/libstdc++ build classic happens to run on, not of MOSES's seeding contract. `_CStdlibRand` reproduces THIS host's; a classic binary built against a different libc would shuffle differently and this port would then be wrong in the same way its own oracle would be. The fixture's manifest records the toolchain (schema 2) precisely so that shows up as drift. Second, C's `rand()` state is per-PROCESS and classic runs one process per `moses` invocation, whereas `_cstdlib_rand()` below is a per-INTERPRETER singleton. Any in-process harness that drives the CLI more than once must call `_cstdlib_rand().srand(1)` between runs, exactly as CARRY-FORWARD.md 5.11 requires for the reduction singletons. Every differential sweep in this project uses a fresh subprocess per run and is unaffected. |

| pymoses/util/tree.py | 35 | C++ opencog/util/tree.h:111 | no head/feet sentinels -- a single root is tracked directly as `self._root`. Consequence: `insert`, `insert_after`, `move_after`, and `move_before` all raise ValueError if asked to give the tree's own root a new sibling (or to move the root itself into a sibling position), since either would require a forest (multiple top-level roots), which this port does not support. In C++ these same calls quietly succeed by growing a momentary or permanent forest under head/feet. `move_ontop(root, source)` is rejected too, but for a different reason: the C++ precondition forbids the target from containing the source, and violating it destroys the target subtree before reading the now-freed source (undefined behaviour), rather than creating a forest. `insert_above` and `wrap` are unaffected -- they replace the root in place rather than adding a sibling of it, which stays well-defined without sentinels. |
| pymoses/util/tree.py | 64 | C++ opencog/util/tree.h:1397 | subtree-copy shared mutable data; now copies it. Found in review (task-3, S7), not a deliberate design: `tree.__init__`'s iterator constructor, `append_child`'s and `replace`'s subtree-copy branches, `insert_subtree`/`insert_subtree_after`, and `_copy_descendants`'s own walk all passed a source node's `.data` straight through unwrapped, silently sharing one mutable `argument` object between the original node and every copy of it. C++ copies `vertex` BY VALUE at every one of those call sites, so two different copies can never influence each other by negating one in place -- exactly what `logical_rules.cc:204`/`logical_rules.h:235`'s "copy a subtree, then negate the copy's own root" idiom (`push_back_negated_arguments`, Task 4) depends on. Demonstrated live before this fix: `combo_tree(some_node_holding_$1)` followed by negating the COPY's root argument also flipped the ORIGINAL node's sign (`and($1 $2)` became `and(!$1 $2)`, not just the copy) -- see task-3-report.md's aliasing-root-cause section for the full before/after trace and the regression tests this fix added. `copy.copy` is applied uniformly rather than special-cased per alternative: for this port's three IMMUTABLE `vertex` alternatives (`builtin` -- an IntEnum member, `contin_t` -- a bare `float`, `enum_t`/plain `str`), `copy.copy` is already a documented no-op returning the SAME object (verified: `copy.copy(3.0) is 3.0`), so this costs nothing for the common case and only ever does real work for the one mutable alternative, `argument` (`copy.copy(argument(5))` returns a genuinely distinct object with the same `idx`, confirmed directly). |
| pymoses/util/tree.py | 1119 | C++ opencog/util/tree.h:1239-1242 | classic raises tree_assert when set_head is called on a nonempty tree; Python raises ValueError and guarantees the existing root is untouched. |
| pymoses/util/tree.py | 1380 | C++ opencog/util/tree.h:805 | C++ destroys and deallocates the erased `tree_node` (tree.h:824-825) after unlinking it -- any pointer a caller kept is now dangling, and dereferencing it is undefined behaviour (typically a crash, sometimes silent corruption). Python cannot reproduce "dangling": the object stays alive as long as anything references it, so a naive port would leave its outward links holding their stale pre-erase values -- a stale handle would then silently navigate back into the LIVE tree instead of crashing. That is exactly the hazard S8's knob_mapper (one retained iterator per knob) must not hit: ported knob code would silently reattach where C++ crashes. Poisoning those three links with `_DEAD` below makes the failure loud (`AttributeError`, from `_DEAD`'s empty `__slots__`) at the first attempt to navigate through them -- safer than C++'s silent corruption, but a deliberate, disclosed divergence from it, not an equivalent implementation of the same dangling-pointer behaviour. |
| pymoses/util/tree.py | 1453 | C++ opencog/util/tree.h:1397 | this subtree-copy branch destroys and deallocates `position` and its old descendants; C++'s own docs therefore call the iterator "invalid". Any retained C++ handle is dangling and reading it is undefined behaviour. Python retains referenced objects, so the discarded node and all former descendants are poisoned with `_DEAD` instead: a loud, deterministic failure rather than undefined behaviour. |
| pymoses/util/tree.py | 1624 | C++ opencog/util/tree.h:1708 | inherited upstream defect, deliberately not fixed here. move_ontop(target, source) corrupts the sibling chain when `source` is `target`'s immediate next sibling: `b_next_sibling` is captured as target.next_sibling *before* target is erased, and when source IS that next sibling, the final `source.next_sibling = b_next_sibling` sets source.next_sibling to source itself -- a self-cycle -- while `parent.last_child` is left stale (never corrected). A traversal that later follows next_sibling through this node -- including this class's own __iter__ -- does not terminate, in Python exactly as in C++. This is deliberate fidelity to a real upstream bug (confirmed against tools/cpp_probe/tree_probe.cc's move_ontop_adjacent_sibling_bug case and test_move_ontop_adjacent_sibling_self_cycle_is_an_inherited_bug), not an oversight. DO NOT "FIX" THIS. The correct-looking change -- special-case `target.next_sibling is source` and relink correctly, or assert against it -- would silently diverge this port from the C++ baseline it exists to mirror. If upstream tree.h is ever corrected, port that fix here in the same change, not before, and update the regression test above to match. Restores `tree.h:1714-1715`'s `tree_assert(dst); tree_assert(src);` (null checks), and enforces tree.h:1708's documented precondition that source is not contained in target's subtree. All guards run BEFORE mutation, so a rejected call leaves the tree untouched. |
